\verb!length! field and a number of indexed fields starting at
\verb!0!, and that we can access these fields using square bracket
notation. Thus treat the length and fields of an array as its
structure, and use the same structural compatibility checks for the
existence of array elements that we do for the existence of fields in
a object.  Thus, we would not, for example, grow an array to allow a
constraint on its $i^{th}$ element to be satisfied if it didn't
already have an $i^{th}$ element. If we want to express that all
fields in such an array should be pairwise different, we can do so in
Babelsberg/Objects with a loop:
\begin{lstlisting}
i := 0;
while i < array.length do (
  j := 0;
  while j < array.length do (
    if i != j then always array[i?] != array[j?]
    j := j + 1
  );
  i := i + 1
)
\end{lstlisting}
Such a constraint could be used, for example, in the implementation of
a Sudoku game~\cite{lynce2006sudoku}, to express the constraint that
numbers must not repeat per cell, line, or column.

The above code has two main problems, however. First, if we consider
collections that can grow (or shrink), these constraints would then be
incorrect --- they would either have to be redacted and the loops
re-executed or after adding the above constraints once any changes to
the size of the collection must be prohibited. Second, many
object-oriented languages include a set of \acp{api} to work with
collections, and rather than iterating manually, a method such as
\verb!all_elements_different!, if available on the \verb!Array! class,
should work in a constraint:
\begin{lstlisting}
always array.all_elements_different()
\end{lstlisting}

There are a number of collection predicates that are commonly used in
constraints and that would be useful to support. For these, we propose
to check their actual implementation and allow them in a modified form
of \gls{ccm}. In this mode, rather than simply executing through
complex methods involving loops, we convert any operations involving
array elements that are used as tests into constraints. Any indexing
variable is treated as read-only. If the test would trigger an early
return, we ignore the return and continue.

Depending on the type of recognized test, the generated constraints
must then added to a conjunction or disjunction. The system determines
whether to use a conjunction or disjunction if the method uses an
early return optimization. If, depending on an element test, the
method would early return \verb!true!, the tests must be combined in a
disjunction, since it is enough to satisfy just one test to have the
method return the same. Otherwise, the elements are negated and added
in to a conjunction.  Thus, an implementation of
\verb!all_elements_different!  with the following implementation would
be turned into a conjunction of pair-wise inequality constraints:
\begin{lstlisting}
def all_elements_different()
  i := 0;
  while i < self.length do (
    j := 0;
    while j < self.length do (
      if i != j && self[i] = self[j] then return false;
      j := j + 1
    );
    i := i + 1
  );
  return true
end
\end{lstlisting}
The above code would generate a conjunction of constraints, because
the early return is \verb!false!. The constraints in the conjunction
would be for the tests to that early return, pair-wise constraining
\verb!array[i] = array[j]! to be false (with the values of \verb!i!
and \verb!j! fixed for each constraint).

\begin{table}
  \caption{Mapping from collection predicates to declarative representation}
  \label{tab:mapping-collection}
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \verb!every! & $\forall x\in \texttt{array}. f(x)$\\
    \verb!some! & $\exists x\in \texttt{array}. f(x)$\\
    \verb!member! & $y. \exists x\in \texttt{array}. x = y$\\
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Some common predicates available on collections in Common Lisp are
translated as per \autoref{tab:mapping-collection}. % Note that both the
% test if some element satisfies a particular predicate as well as the
% test for membership (the latter being a special case of the former)
% require a disjunction. Without any other constraints, and since our
% design does not use Prolog-style backtracking, they would probably
% always be satisfied by setting the first (or last, depending on the
% concrete implementation) element of the array.
Even though this list contains only a few predicates, in practice many
languages come only with a relatively small set of collection types
that are supported at a language level. Languages with a rich
collection library such as Common Lisp or Squeak/Smalltalk~\cite{Ingalls1997Squeak} are built around a small number of types and
primitive operations to access and store indexed elements in an
object. Thus, implementing the special support needed to support these
basic predicates enables their use in a variety of contexts, including
methods that are built on top of these predicates. Examples for these
are the Common Lisp methods \verb!notevery! which simply uses a
negation of the predicate used in \verb!some! or \verb!notany!, which
is the negation for \verb!every!.

\subsection{Constraints on User-Defined Methods}
\label{sec:constr-user-methods}

We do not intend to support every possible method that a collection
may have in a practical implementation, in particular if that
collection may be extended with user-defined methods. As an example,
consider an iterative \verb|sum| method:
\begin{lstlisting}
def sum()
  answer := 0;
  i := 0;
  while i < self.length (
    answer := answer + self[i];
    i := i + 1
  );
  return ans
end
\end{lstlisting}
We can of course use this method in the forward direction in a
constraint, according to the rules in
\autoref{sec:additional-constraint-restrictions}:
\begin{lstlisting}
a := new Array(2);
a[0] := 10;
a[1] := 20;
s := 30;
always s = a.sum();
a[0] := 100
\end{lstlisting}
After the \verb|always| constraint is executed, \verb|s| is 30; then
after the final assignment to \verb|a[0]|, \verb|s| becomes 120.
However, the method doesn't work backwards --- for example, we can't
constrain the sum of the array and expect the system to update one or
more elements to satisfy the constraint.  So the constraint in the
last line below will be too hard for the system to solve:
\begin{lstlisting}
a := new Array(2);
a[0] := 10;
a[1] := 20;
always 50 = a.sum()
\end{lstlisting}

We have found a design pattern for user code that works well in these
situations that \emph{can} provide something that works both forward
and backward. Rather than using a method that returns the calculated
sum of the elements, we eagerly update the sum as the array changes in
a variable. This can be done by writing an ordinary method that sets
up the appropriate network of addition constraints. The \verb|sum|
becomes an instance variable of our collection, and the implementation
of that collection must take care to correctly initialize the
constraint network when it is created or an element is added or
removed:
\begin{lstlisting}
def initialize_sum()
  if self.length = 0 then
    always self.sum = 0
  else (
    sub_array := new Array(self.length - 1);
    i := 1;
    while i < self.length do (
      sub_array[i - 1] := self[i];
      always sub_array[i - 1] = self[i];
      i := i + 1
    )
    always self.sum = self[0] + sub_array.sum
  )
end
\end{lstlisting}
The advantage for code using the collection's sum in further
constraints is that it is used simply as a variable --- constraints on
it can work both ways, and it can even be assigned and the array
changes to satisfy the constraints. We will discuss additional useful
design patterns such as this in \autoref{part:pract-object-constr}.

% ~4 pages
% - collection apis
% - sets are a nice declarative feature
% - objects usually don't care what they are in
% - need to worry about stuff like early return optimizations
% - collections implemented on top of each other, but breaks down to
% complex objects problem

\chapter{Semantics}
\label{sec:semantics}

This chapter presents a complete semantics of our Babelsberg design
that can be used to guide practical implementations~\cite{FelgentreffOOPSLA2015}. It is meant to as simple as possible,
while still encompassing the major aspects of \gls{ocp} and the
important design decisions in its Babelsberg form. Because Babelsberg
integrates constraints in an existing object-constraint host language,
the semantics omits constructs such as exception handling for
constraint solver failures, class and method definitions, and
syntactic sugar, that are intended to be inherited from the host
language. Our semantics instead focuses on the expression of standard
object-oriented constructs that need to be modified to support the
Babelsberg design. We present the semantics in the same increments as
we did the design. First, \autoref{sec:primitive-types} presents the
semantics of a simple imperative language that has only primitive
reals, integers, and booleans. Second, \autoref{sec:constr-with-mess}
adds the rules for method lookup and dispatch, identity constraints,
and the interaction with the heap, and presents theorems for key
properties, proofs for which have been previously published~\cite{felgentreff:2014:semantics}. Third,
\autoref{sec:records-with-ident} presents the rules for constraints
over collections.

Finally, \autoref{sec:exec-spec} presents an executable form of the
complete semantics that passes a language test suite. We use this
executable form to demonstrate the validity of our rules, and provide
a mechanism to generate test suites that can be run against Babelsberg
implementations to assert their compliance to the semantics.

\section{Primitive Types}
\label{sec:primitive-types}

\renewcommand{\Scope}{E\xspace}

We start with the basic language Babelsberg/PrimitiveTypes that has
only primitive values. The boolean type is required to make meaningful
Babelsberg programs, since by definition constraint expressions must
return either true or false (with the solvers task to make them
true). For this initial language, we also add reals, integers, and
strings.

\subsection{Syntax}
\label{sec:babelsberg-primitivetypes-syntax}

{\texttt{
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\textrm{Statement} & s & ::= & skip $\mid$ x := e $\mid$ always C $\mid$ once C $\mid$ s;s \\
  & & & $\mid$ if e then s else s $\mid$ while e do s \\
\textrm{Constraint} & C & ::= & $\rho$ e $\mid$ C $\wedge$ C\\
\textrm{Expression} & e & ::= & c $\mid$ x $\mid$ e $\oplus$ e\\
\textrm{Constant} & c & ::= & true $\mid$ false $\mid$ \textrm{base type constants}\\
\textrm{Variable} & x & ::= & \textrm{variable names} \\ & & &\\
\textrm{Value} & v & ::= & c
\end{tabular}
}}\\[\baselineskip]

The language includes a set of boolean and base type constants (\eg reals), ranged over by metavariable \texttt{c}. A
finite set of operators on expressions is ranged over by $\oplus$. This includes
operations on the reals such as \texttt{+} and \texttt{*},
a set of {\em predicate} operators (\texttt{$=$} and \texttt{$\neq$},
$\leq$, $<$, $=$, and so on. It also includes a set of logical operators for combining
boolean expressions (\eg \texttt{$\wedge$}, \texttt{$\vee$}).  The
predicate operations are assumed to include at least an equality
operator \texttt{=} for each primitive type in the language, and the
logical operations are assumed to include at least conjunction \texttt{$\wedge$}.
The syntax of this language does have some limitations as compared
with that of a practical language --- for example, there are only
binary operators (not unary or ternary), and the result must have the
same type as the arguments.  We make these simplifications since the
purpose of Babelsberg/PrimitiveTypes is to elucidate the semantics of
such languages as a step toward Babelsberg/Objects, rather than to
specify a real language.

For constraints, the symbol $\rho$ ranges over a finite and totally
ordered set of constraint {\em priorities} and is assumed to include a
bottom element \texttt{ weak}
% , an intermediate element {\em strong},
and a top element \texttt{ required}. While syntax requires the priority to be
explicit, for simplicity we sometimes omit it in this semantics. A
constraint with no explicit priority implicitly has the priority \texttt{
  required}.  Finally, for simplicity we do not model read-only
annotations in the formal semantics.
%% commented out 'strong' since I don't think we make use of it in the
%% formal semantics

The syntax is thus that of a simple, 
standard imperative language except for the
\texttt{always} and \texttt{once} statements, which declare constraints.
An \texttt{always} constraint must
hold for the rest of the programs execution, whereas a \texttt{once}
constraint is satisfied by the solver and then retracted. 
%
Note that for simplicity this semantics implicitly gets stuck whenever
the solver cannot satisfy a constraint, either due to an unsatisfiable
constraint or due to the solver being unable to determine whether the
constraint is satisfiable. In a practical implementation, we would
likely want to differentiate between 
these cases, since it's useful if we can
inform the programmer that the constraints are truly not satisfiable.
We could also add standard exception handling to remove the
unsatisfiable or unknown constraint and continue, but omit this here
for simplicity.

\subsection{Semantics}

\begin{table*}
  \caption{Judgments and Intuitions of Semantic Rules}
  \label{tab:prim-judgments}
  \centering
  \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{r X}
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Expression Evaluation}}}\\
    \eval{\Scope}{e}{v} & Expression \texttt{e} evaluates to value \texttt{v} in the context of environment \texttt{\Scope}.\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Constraint Solving}}}\\
    \solve{\Scope}{C} & Environment \Scope represents a solution to constraint \texttt{C} \\
    \gstay{x=v}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a weak stay constraint for \texttt{x} to equal \texttt{v}\\
    \gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a conjunction of stay constraints on variables in environment \Scope \\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Statement Evaluation}}}\\
    \step{\Scope}{C}{s}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$} & Execution starting from configuration \texttt{<\Scope|C|s>} ends in state \texttt{<\Scope$'$|C$'$>}.\\
    % \stepH{\E}{\H}{\C}{\I}{s}{\E$'$}{\H$'$}{\C$'$}{\I$'$} & Execution starting from configuration \texttt{<\E|\H|\C|\I|s>} ends in the state \texttt{<E$'$|\H$'$|C$'$|\I$'$>}\\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

The semantics is defined by several judgments, given in
\autoref{tab:prim-judgments}.  These judgments depend on the notion of an {\em
  environment}, which is a partial function from program variables to program
values. Metavariable \texttt{\Scope} ranges over environments. When convenient
we also view an environment as a set of (program variable, program value) pairs.
For each operator $\oplus$ in the language we assume the existence of a
corresponding semantic function denoted $\llbracket \oplus \rrbracket$.

% \judgment{\eval{\Scope}{e}{v}}

% ``Expression \texttt{e} evaluates to value \texttt{v} in the context of environment \texttt{
%   \Scope}.''

\inferax{E-Const}{\eval{\Scope}{c}{c}}

\inferrule{E-Var}{
  \texttt{\Scope(x) = v}
}{
  \eval{\Scope}{x}{v}
}


% \inferrule{E-ReadOnly}{
%   \texttt{\Scope(x) = v}
% }{
%   \eval{\Scope}{x?}{v}
% }

\inferrule{E-Op}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$}{v$_1$} \andalso
  \eval{\Scope}{e$_2$}{v$_2$} \andalso
  \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \oplus \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}
}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$ $\oplus$ e$_2$}{v}
}

% \inferrule{E-Compare}{
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$}{v$_1$} \andalso
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_2$}{v$_2$} \andalso
%   \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \olessthan \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}
% }{
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$ $\olessthan$ e$_2$}{v}
% }

% \inferrule{E-Combine}{
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$}{v$_1$} \andalso
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_2$}{v$_2$} \andalso
%   \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \ovee \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}
% }{
%   \eval{\Scope}{e$_1$ $\ovee$ e$_2$}{v}
% }

% \judgment{\solve{\Scope}{C}}

Solving is represented by a call to the constraint solver, which we treat as a
black box.  The proposition \mbox{\solve{\Scope}{C}} denotes that environment
\texttt{\Scope} is a {\em solution} to the constraint \texttt{C} (and further
one that is optimal according to the solver's semantics, as discussed earlier).

% \judgment{\gstay{x=v}{$\rho$}{C}}
% \judgment{\gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{C}}

Our helper judgments \mbox{\gstay{x=v}{$\rho$}{C}} and
\mbox{\gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{C}} define how to translate an environment into a
source-level ``stay'' constraint. They take a priority as an argument, which is
not used at this stage of the semantics, but will be used in all the later
stages.

\infrule[StayEnv]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{\Scope} = \texttt{\{(x$_1$, v$_1$), $\ldots$, (x$_n$, v$_n$)\}}}\\
  \mbox{
    \gstay{x$_1$=v$_1$}{$\rho$}{C$_1$} $\cdots$   \gstay{x$_n$=v$_n$}{$\rho$}{C$_n$}}
}{\mbox{
  \gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{\texttt{C}$_1$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ \texttt{C}$_n$}
}}

\inferax{StayConst}{\gstay{x=c}{$\rho$}{weak x=c}}

% \judgment{\step{\Scope}{C}{s}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}}

% ``Execution starting from configuration \texttt{<\Scope|C|s>} ends in state \texttt{<\Scope$'$|C$'$>}.''

The rules for evaluating statements are given below. A ``configuration''
defining the state of an execution includes a concrete context, represented by
the environment, a symbolic context, represented by the constraint, and a
statement to be executed.  The environment and statement are standard, while the
constraint is not part of the state of a computation in most languages.
Intuitively, the environment comes from constraint solving during the evaluation
of the immediately preceding statement, and the constraint records the
\verb|always| constraints that have been declared so far during execution. Note
that our execution implicitly gets stuck if the solver cannot produce a model.

\inferrule{S-Asgn}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e}{v} \andalso
  \gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{C$_s$} \andalso
  \solve{\Scope$'$}{(C $\wedge$ C$_s$ $\wedge$ x = v)}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{x := e}{\Scope$'$}{C}
}

\inferrule{S-Once}{
  \gstay{\Scope}{$\rho$}{C$_s$} \andalso
  \solve{\Scope$'$}{(C $\wedge$ C$_s$ $\wedge$ C$_0$)}
}
{\step{\Scope}{C}{once C$_0$}{\Scope$'$}{C}}

\inferrule{S-Always}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{once C$_0$}{\Scope$'$}{C} \andalso \texttt{C$'$ = C $\wedge$ C$_0$}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{always C$_0$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}
}

\inferax{S-Skip}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{skip}{\Scope}{C}
} 

\inferrule{S-Seq}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{s$_1$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$} \andalso
  \step{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}{s$_2$}{\Scope$''$}{C$''$}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{s$_1$;s$_2$}{\Scope$''$}{C$''$}
}

\inferrule{S-IfThen}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e}{true} \andalso
  \step{\Scope}{C}{s$_1$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{if e then s$_1$ else s$_2$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}%
}

\inferrule{S-IfElse}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e}{false} \andalso
  \step{\Scope}{C}{s$_2$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{if e then s$_1$ else s$_2$}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}%
}

\inferrule{S-WhileDo}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e}{true} \andalso
  \step{\Scope}{C}{s}{\Scope$'$}{C$'$} \andalso
  \step{\Scope$'$}{C$'$}{while e do s}{\Scope$''$}{C$''$}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{while e do s}{\Scope$''$}{C$''$}
}

\inferrule{S-WhileSkip}{
  \eval{\Scope}{e}{false}
}{
  \step{\Scope}{C}{while e do s}{\Scope}{C}
}

\section{Objects and Messages}
\label{sec:constr-with-mess}

\renewcommand{\Scope}{\ensuremath{\mathbb{S}}\xspace}

The solver must now also handle objects which are modeled as simple
records.  To tame the power of the solver so that it adheres to the
principles presented in \ref{sec:design}, we add the two-phase
solving, structural type checks, and inlining rules on constraints
described in \ref{sec:determinism}. These structural compatibility
checks are assertions that are checked dynamically before sending the
constraints involving objects to the solver, for example, checking
whether a variable is bound to an object, and whether the object has
the necessary fields.  While these assertions are checked, unlike
constraints the system will never change anything to enforce them ---
if one is violated it's just an error.  Instead, the programmer must
ensure that an object with the expected fields is first assigned to a
variable used in object constraints, just as a programmer would need
to ensure that an object with the expected fields was assigned to a
object-valued variable in a standard language.

The semantic rules are still mostly standard imperative rules, with
the addition of rules to assert and maintain constraints. The
semantics for this language, although omitting collections and
cooperating constraint solvers, illustrates the key principles we want
for an \ac{ocp} language. The semantics still treats the solver as a
black box, but we assume it supports our primitive types, records,
uninterpreted functions, as well as hard and soft constraints~\cite{borning1992constraint}. Many practical examples use read-only
variables, but this is not relevant for this semantics.

\subsection{Syntax}

The syntax is augmented to support creating instances of objects (both
for value classes and ordinary classes) as well as method
invocation. We introduce syntax for the method body.

{\texttt{
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\textrm{Statement} & s & ::= & skip $\mid$ L := e $\mid$ always C $\mid$ once C $\mid$ s;s \\
  & & & $\mid$ if e then s else s $\mid$ while e do s \\
\textrm{Constraint} & C & ::= & $\rho$ e $\mid$ C $\wedge$ C\\
\textrm{Expression} & e & ::= & v $\mid$ L $\mid$ e $\oplus$ e $\mid$ I \\
%$\mid$ e $\olessthan$ e $\mid$ e $\ovee$ e \\
 & & & $\mid$ e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$) $\mid$ o $\mid$ new o $\mid$ D\\
\textrm{Identity} & I & ::= & e == e\\
\textrm{Object Literal} & o & ::= &  \{l$_1$:e$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$_n$\} \\
\textrm{L-Value} & L & ::= & x $\mid$ e.l \\
\textrm{Constant} & c & ::= & true $\mid$ false $\mid$ nil $\mid$ \textrm{base type constants}\\
\textrm{Variable} & x & ::= & \textrm{variable names} \\
\textrm{Label} & l & ::= & \textrm{record label names} \\
\textrm{Reference} & r & ::= & \textrm{references to heap records} \\
\textrm{Dereference} & D & ::= & \Deref(e) \\ & & &\\
\textrm{Method Body} & b & ::= & s; return e $\mid$ return e \\
\textrm{Value} & v & ::= & c $\mid$ r $\mid$ \{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}
\end{tabular}
}}\\[\baselineskip]

The syntax elements \texttt{c}, $\rho$, and $\oplus$ are mostly
unchanged.  We add the operator \texttt{==}, which tests for identity
--- for primitive values this behaves the same as \texttt{=}. We add
labels for naming fields, and syntax for referencing them. Since we
now have a heap, in the syntax, we treat \Deref as a keyword used for
dereferencing.  Source programs will not use expressions of the form
\texttt{\Deref(e)}, but they are introduced as part of constraints
given to the solver, which we assume will treat \Deref as an
uninterpreted function. We also assume that the solver supports
records and record equality, which we also denote with the \texttt{=}
operator. We furthermore assume that the set of variable names ranged
over by metavariable \texttt{x} includes the name \texttt{self}.

\subsection{Semantics}

\begin{sidewaystable*}
  \centering
  \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{r X}
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Method Invocation}}}\\
    \lookup{v}{l}{b} & Lookup of method \texttt{l} in the object or value \texttt{v} returns the
                       formal parameter names \texttt{x$_1$} through \texttt{x$_n$} and the method
                       body \texttt{b} \\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\enter{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}}\\
                     & Invoking a method on \texttt{v} with argument names \texttt{x$_1$} through
                       \texttt{x$_n$} and arguments \texttt{e$_1$} through \texttt{e$_n$} constructs
                       the method scope \texttt{\Scope$_m$} and may update the global state.\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Expression Evaluation}}}\\
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}
                     & Evaluating expression \texttt{e} produces the value \texttt{v} and updated state \Env$'$, \Heap$'$, \Cstore$'$, and \Istore$'$\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Typechecking}}}\\
    \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e}{T} & Expression \texttt{e} has type \texttt{T} in the context of environment
                              \texttt{\Env} and heap \Heap\\
    \tcHC{\Env}{\Heap}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is well formed in the context of environment
                            \texttt{\Env} and heap \Heap \\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Constraint Solving}}}\\
    \solveH{\Env}{\Heap}{C} & Environment \Env and heap \Heap represent a solution to constraint \texttt{C} \\
    \gstay{\Env}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a conjunction of stay constraints on variables in environment \Env \\
    \gstay{\Heap}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a conjunction of stay constraints on objects in heap \Heap \\
    \gstay{x=c}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a weak stay constraint for \texttt{x} to equal \texttt{c}\\
    \gstay{x=r}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a stay constraint with priority $\rho$ for \texttt{x} to equal \texttt{r}\\
    \gstay{r=o}{$\rho$}{C} & Constraint \texttt{C} is a required stay constraint for reference \texttt{r} to refer to the object \texttt{o}\\
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$} & Inlining expression \texttt{e} in \texttt{\Scope} is equivalent to
                                                                              \texttt{e$'$} in \Env if \texttt{e$_C$} evaluates to true.\\
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{\Env$'$}{C} & Re-inlining the constraint store \Cstore\ returns a
                                                                   constraint \texttt{C}\\
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\Env$'$}{C} & Re-inlining the constraint store \Istore\ returns a
                                                                   constraint \texttt{C}\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Statement Evaluation}}}\\
    % \multicolumn{2}{l}{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}% }\\
                     & Execution starting from state \texttt{<\Env|\Scope|\Heap|\Cstore|\Istore|s>} ends in
                       \texttt{<\Env$'$|\Scope$'$|\Heap$'$|\Cstore$'$|\Istore$'$>}\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Helper Rules}}}\\
    \solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$} & Solving identity constraints and \texttt{$\rho$ e} yields new environment and heap \Env$'$ and \Heap$'$\\
    \solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$} & Solving value constraints and \texttt{$\rho$ e} yields new environment and heap \Env$'$ and \Heap$'$\\
  \end{tabularx}
  \caption{Judgments and Intuitions of Semantic Rules for Objects}
  \label{tab:obj-judgments}
\end{sidewaystable*}

The semantics now include a global \Env\ and a heap \Heap, in addition to method
local scopes \Scope. The first is a function that maps global variable names to
values, the second is a function that maps mutable references to ``objects'' of
the form \texttt{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}, and the third now maps
local variable names to global variable names. When convenient, we also treat
both \Env\ and \Heap\ as a set of pairs (\texttt{\{(x,v),...\}} and
\texttt{\{(r,o),...\}}, respectively). The currently active value constraints
are kept as a compound constraint \Cstore; identity constraints are kept as a
single conjunction referred to as \Istore.

This semantics requires updated and additional judgments given in
\autoref{tab:obj-judgments}. The rules for expression evaluation still work on
the local scope, but since a local scope is now only a mapping from local names
to globally unique names, the rules are augmented to then look up the value in
the global environment. Method lookup creates new local environments, and
constraints are translated to translate local variable names to global variable
names for the solver. The solver still returns a global environment, and does
not know about the local environments and their mapping into the global
environment. In addition, the constraint stores \texttt{I} and \texttt{C} are
replaced with \Istore\ and \Cstore, respectively. These now store constraints in
pairs with the local environment they were created in. For readability, we now
write \Heap\ instead of \texttt{H}, so all the global stores are written in the
same font.

% \judgment{\lookup{v}{l}{b}}

% ``Lookup of method \texttt{l} in the object or value \texttt{v} returns the
% formal parameter names \texttt{x$_1$} through \texttt{x$_n$} and the method
% body \texttt{b}''

% \judgment{\enter{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}}

% ``Invoking a method on \texttt{v} with argument names \texttt{x$_1$} through
% \texttt{x$_n$} and arguments \texttt{e$_1$} through \texttt{e$_n$} constructs
% the method scope \texttt{\Scope$_m$} and may update the heap and constraint stores.''

The judgment for method lookup is opaque: our semantics does not depend on how
method lookup is performed. After lookup we create a fresh scope with the
evaluated arguments bound to the parameters of the method, the rule for which is
given below:

\infrule[Enter]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}{v$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{$\cdots$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_{n-1}$}{\Cstore$_{n-1}$}{\Istore$_{n-1}$}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{v$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$_n$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{self := v}{\Env$_0$}{\Scope$_0$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$_0$}{\Scope$_0$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{x$_1$ := v$_1$}{\Env$_{n+1}$}{\Scope$_1$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{$\cdots$}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$_{2n-1}$}{\Scope$_{n-1}$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{x$_n$ := v$_n$}{\Env$_{2n}$}{\Scope$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}}
}{\mbox{
    \enter{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$_{2n}$}{\Scope$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}
}}

% \judgment{\evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}}

% ``Evaluating expression \texttt{e} produces the value \texttt{v}, while
% possibly having side-effects on everything but the local environment.''

Expression evaluation can now have effects on the global environment, heap, and
the constraint stores (but not on the local scope), because calling methods is
possible in expressions.

\inferax{E-Const}{\evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{c}{\Env}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{c}}

\inferrule{E-Var}{
  \texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{x$_g$} \andalso
  \Env(\texttt{x$_g$}) = \texttt{v}
}{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x}{\Env}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}
}

\infrule[E-Field]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{r} \andalso
    \texttt{\Heap$'$}(\texttt{r}) = \texttt{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}}\\
  \mbox{1 $\leq$ $i$ $\leq$ $n$}
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l$_i$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_i$}
}}

\inferrule{E-ValueField}{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}} \andalso
  1 $\leq$ $i$ $\leq$ $n$
}{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l$_i$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_i$}
}

\inferax{E-Ref}{\evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{r}{\Env}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{r}}

\infrule[E-Op]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_2$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \oplus \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}}
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\oplus$ e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}
}}

% \infrule[E-Compare]{
%   \mbox{
%     \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v$_1$}}\\
%   \mbox{
%     \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_2$}}\\
%   \mbox{
%     \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \olessthan \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}}
% }{\mbox{
%   \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\olessthan$ e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}
% }}

% \infrule[E-Combine]{
%   \mbox{
%     \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v$_1$}}\\
%   \mbox{
%     \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_2$}}\\
%   \mbox{
%     \texttt{v$_1$ $\llbracket \ovee \rrbracket$ v$_2$} = \texttt{v}}
% }{\mbox{
%   \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\ovee$ e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}
% }}


\infrule[E-IdentityTrue]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{e$_2$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}}
}{\mbox{%
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ == e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{true}
}}

\infrule[E-IdentityFalse]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{e$_2$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_2$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{v$_1$} $\neq$ \texttt{v$_2$}}
}{\mbox{%
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ == e$_2$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{false}
}}

\infrule[E-Call]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \lookup{v}{l}{s; return e}}\\
  \mbox{
    \enter{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v}{\Env$_1$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{\stepHO{\Env$_1$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}{s}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{e}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}{v$_r$}}
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}{v$_r$}
}}

\infrule[E-CallSimple]{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{\lookup{v}{l}{return e}}\\
\mbox{
  \enter{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_0$}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore$_0$}{v}{\Env$_1$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}}\\
\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env$_1$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_r$}}
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v$_r$}
}}

\infrule[E-New]{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}{v$_1$}}\\
\mbox{$\cdots$}\\
\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_{n-1}$}{\Cstore$_{n-1}$}{\Istore$_{n-1}$}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{v$_n$}}\\
\mbox{
\texttt{\Heap$_n$}(\texttt{r})$\uparrow$ \andalso  \texttt{\Heap$'$} = (\texttt{\Heap$_n$} $\bigcup$ \{(\texttt{r}, \texttt{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}})\})}
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{new \{l$_1$:e$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$_n$\}}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{r}
}}

\infrule[E-Value]{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$}{v$_1$}}\\\mbox{$\cdots$}\\\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap$_{n-1}$}{\Cstore$_{n-1}$}{\Istore$_{n-1}$}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{v$_n$}}\\
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\{l$_1$:e$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$_n$\}}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap$_n$}{\Cstore$_n$}{\Istore$_n$}{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}
}}

% \judgment{\tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e}{T}}
% \judgment{\tcHC{\Env}{\Heap}{C}}

We now typecheck expressions and constraints with respect to the global
environments \Env$\,$.  Method calls do not typecheck: even though we allow
method calls in expressions and thus in constraints syntactically, our rules for
creating constraints given below inline method invocations.  Identity
constraints also do not typecheck: they are solved separately and should not
appear in ordinary constraints (see the rules for statement evaluation below).
Finally, new (non-value) object construction does not typecheck, since
constraints must be side-effect-free.

Types distinguish between primitive values and objects, and for
objects the type keeps track of their fields:

{\texttt{
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\textrm{Type} & T & ::= & PrimitiveType $\mid$
\{l$_1$:T$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:T$_n$\} 
\end{tabular}
}}

\inferax{T-Const}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{c}{PrimitiveType}
}


\inferrule{T-Ref}{
  \texttt{\Heap}(\texttt{r})=\texttt{o} \andalso
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{o}{T} 
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{r}{T}
}

\inferrule{T-Var}{
  \Env(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{v} \andalso
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{v}{T}
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{x}{T}
}

We now transform all local variables to their global names using the
inlining rules given below before passing them to the solver. Thus,
only global variable names type.

\inferrule{T-Field}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e}{\{l$_1$:T$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:T$_n$\}}
  \andalso $1 \leq i \leq n$
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e.l$_i$}{T$_i$}
}

We add a typing rule for dereferences. We assume dereferences will not
appear in source programs, but only in expressions that have been
generated by our inlining judgment. This ensures that the inlined
expressions still typecheck, and simplifies the rules for constraint
solving.

\inferrule{T-Deref}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e}{T}
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{\Deref(e)}{T}
}

\inferrule{T-Op}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$}{PrimitiveType} \andalso \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$ $\oplus$ e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
}

% \inferrule{T-Compare}{
%   \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$}{PrimitiveType} \andalso \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
% }{
%   \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$ $\olessthan$ e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
% }

% \inferrule{T-Combine}{
%   \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$}{PrimitiveType} \andalso \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
% }{
%   \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$ $\ovee$ e$_2$}{PrimitiveType}
% }

\inferrule{T-ValueObject}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_1$}{T$_1$} $\cdots$ \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e$_n$}{T$_n$}
}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{\{l$_1$:e$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$_n$\}}{\{l$_1$:T$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:T$_n$\}}
}

\inferrule{T-Priority}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{e}{T}
}{
  {\tcHC{\Env}{\Heap}{$\rho$ e}}
}

\inferrule{T-Conjunction}{
  \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{C$_1$}{T} \andalso \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{C$_2$}{T}
}{
  \tcHC{\Env}{\Heap}{C$_1$ $\wedge$ C$_2$}
}

% \judgment{\gstay{x=v}{$\rho$}{C}}
% \judgment{\gstay{r=o}{$\rho$}{C}}
% \judgment{\gstay{\Env}{$\rho$}{C}}
% \judgment{\gstay{\Heap}{$\rho$}{C}}

The rules for creating stay constraints are simply extended to include rules for
stay constraints on instances of objects, and adapted to this formalism's
environment and heap.

% \inferax{StayEmpty}{\stay{$\emptyset$}{true}}

% \inferrule{StayOne}{
%   \Env$(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{v}$ \andalso
%   \Env$_0 = $\Env$ \setminus \{(\texttt{x},\texttt{v})\}$ \andalso
%   \stay{\Env$_0$}{C$_0$} \andalso
%   \texttt{C}$ = $\texttt{C}$_0$ $\wedge$ \texttt{weak x=v}
% }{
%   \stay{\Env}{C}
% }

% \infrule[StayHeap]{
%   \mbox{
%     \Heap$(\texttt{r}) = \texttt{o}$ \andalso  
%     \Heap$_0 = $\Heap$ \setminus \{(\texttt{r},\texttt{o})\}$ \andalso
%     \stay{\Heap$_0$}{C$_0$} \andalso
%     $\texttt{o} = $\{\texttt{l$_0$:v$_0$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$}\}}\\
%   \mbox{C$_w$ $=$ weak x$_{r0}$ = v$_0$ $\wedge$
%     $\ldots$ $\wedge$
%     weak x$_{rn}$ = v$_n$}
% }{\mbox{
%   \stay{\Heap}{C$_0$ $\wedge$
%     weak \Deref(r) = \{l$_0$:x$_{r0}$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:x$_{rn}$\} $\wedge$ C$_w$}
% }}

% \inferax{IStayEmpty}{\istay{$\emptyset$}{true}}
% \inferax{IStayConst}{\istay{x=c}{weak x=c}}
% \infrule[IStayObject]{
%   \mbox{
%     \istay{x$_{x0}$=v$_0$}{C$_{x0}$} $\cdots$ \istay{x$_{xn}$=v$_n$}{C$_{xn}$} \andalso
%     $\texttt{C}_x = \texttt{C}_{x0} \wedge \ldots \wedge \texttt{C}_{xn}$}
% }{\mbox{
% \istay{x=\{l$_0$:v$_0$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}{
%     \texttt{required x=\{l$_0$:x$_{x0}$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:x$_{xn}$\}} $\wedge$ C$_x$}
% }}
% \inferax{IStayRef}{\istay{x=r}{required x=r}}

% \inferrule{IStayOne}{
%   \Env$(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{v}$ \andalso
%   \Env$_0 = $\Env$ \setminus \{(\texttt{x},\texttt{v})\}$ \andalso
%   \istay{\Env$_0$}{C$_0$} \andalso
%   \istay{x=v}{C$_s$}
% }{
%   \istay{\Env}{\texttt{C}$_0$ $\wedge$ \texttt{C}$_s$}
% }

% \infrule[IStayHeap]{
%   \mbox{
%     \Heap$(\texttt{r}) = \texttt{o}$ \andalso  
%     \Heap$_0 = $\Heap$ \setminus \{(\texttt{r},\texttt{o})\}$ \andalso
%     \istay{\Heap$_0$}{C$_0$} \andalso
%     $\texttt{o} = $\{\texttt{l$_0$:v$_0$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$}\}}\\
%   \mbox{
%     \istay{x$_{r0}$=v$_0$}{C$_{r0}$} $\cdots$ \istay{x$_{rn}$=v$_n$}{C$_{rn}$}}
% }{\mbox{
%   \istay{\Heap}{C$_0$ $\wedge$
%     required \Deref(r) = \{l$_0$:x$_{r0}$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:x$_{rn}$\} $\wedge$
%     C$_{r0}$ $\wedge$
%     $\ldots$ $\wedge$
%     C$_{rn}$ }
% }}

\inferax{StayConst}{\gstay{x=c}{$\rho$}{weak x=c}}

\inferax{StayRef}{\gstay{x=r}{$\rho$}{$\rho$ x=r}}

\infrule[StayRecord]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{ x$_1$} fresh $\cdots$ \texttt{ x$_n$} fresh \andalso 
    \gstay{x$_1$=v$_1$}{$\rho$}{C$_1$} $\cdots$ \gstay{x$_n$=v$_n$}{$\rho$}{C$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{C$ = $($\rho$ x = \{l$_1$:x$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:x$_n$\}) $\wedge$ C$_{1}$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ C$_{n}$}}
}{\mbox{
  \gstay{x = \{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}{$\rho$}{C}
}}

In \textsc{StayRecord} we do not allow records to change their when
solving for value constraints, record structure can only change
through assignments.

\infrule[StayObject]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{ x$_1$} fresh $\cdots$ \texttt{ x$_n$} fresh \andalso 
    \gstay{x$_1$=v$_1$}{$\rho$}{C$_1$} $\cdots$ \gstay{x$_n$=v$_n$}{$\rho$}{C$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{C$ = $(required \Deref(r)=\{l$_1$:x$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:x$_n$\}) $\wedge$ C$_{1}$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ C$_{n}$}}
}{\mbox{
    \gstay{r = \{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}{$\rho$}{C}
}}

\inferrule{StayEnv}{
  \Env = \texttt{\{(x$_1$, v$_1$),$\ldots$,(x$_n$, v$_n$)\}} \andalso
  \gstay{x$_1$=v$_1$}{$\rho$}{C$_1$} $\cdots$   \gstay{x$_n$=v$_n$}{$\rho$}{C$_n$} 
}{
  \gstay{\Env}{$\rho$}{\texttt{C}$_1$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ \texttt{C}$_n$}
}

\infrule[StayHeap]{
  \mbox{
  \Heap = \texttt{\{(r$_1$, o$_1$),$\ldots$,(r$_n$, o$_n$)\}} \andalso
  \gstay{r$_1$=o$_1$}{$\rho$}{C$_1$} $\cdots$ \gstay{r$_n$=o$_n$}{$\rho$}{C$_n$} 
}}
{\mbox{
  \gstay{\Heap}{$\rho$}{C$_1$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ C$_n$}}}



% \judgment{\solveH{\Env}{\Heap}{C}}

As before, the call to the solver is opaque. In this semantics, we assume that
the solver natively supports records and uninterpreted functions (which we use
to represent the heap).  We do not assume, however, that the solver understands
methods, which can now be part of constraint expressions. This requires us to
essentially inline methods before passing constraints to the solver.

% \judgment{\inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$}}

% ``Inlining expression \texttt{e} from the local environment \texttt{\Scope} turns
% into expression \texttt{e$'$}. To connect variables across method calls,
% the constraint expression \texttt{e$_C$} is returned.''

In our inlining rules, we translate local variables into their names in the
global environment and provide a semantics for method calls inside constraints.
Arguments to method calls are constrained to be equal to the expression that
generated them. Inlining does not allow updates to the heap, so no new heap is
returned. We do allow assignments to locals in inlined methods, however, so the
global environment can change as a result of inlining. Since expressions are
actually evaluated during inlining, if they do modify the heap, evaluation stops
and the rules fail.

\inferax{I-Const}{\inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{c}{\Env}{true}{c}}

\inferrule{I-Var}{
  \texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{x$_g$}
}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x}{\Env}{true}{x$_g$}
}

\inferrule{I-Value}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{e$_{C_1}$}{e$'_1$} $\cdots$ \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap$_{n-1}$}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{e$_{C_n}$}{e$'_n$}
}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\{l$_1$:e$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$_n$\}}{\Env$_n$}{e$_{C_1}$$\wedge\cdots\wedge$e$_{C_n}$}{\{l$_1$:e$'_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:e$'_n$\}}
}

\inferrule{I-Field}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$} \andalso
  \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{r}
}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$$\wedge$e$'$=r}{\Deref(e$'$).l}
}

The \textsc{I-Field} rule translates each expression of the form
\texttt{e.l}, where \texttt{e} evaluates to a heap reference
\texttt{r}, into \texttt{\Deref(e$'$).l}
(recursively translating \texttt{e} into \texttt{e$'$}
through further inlining). These judgments further ensure that the
``prefix'' \texttt{e} of an l-value \texttt{e.l} is unchanged through
the addition of the constraint \texttt{e$'$=r};
this is necessary to ensure that updates to the value of \texttt{e.l}
are deterministic. Note that, because we are recursively inlining the
expression before the label, we also recursively add required stays on
any prior field accesses. Thus, for example, \texttt{a.b.c = 1} would
be translated by recursively inlining and returning \texttt{a =
  r$_a$ $\wedge$ H(a).b = r$_b$ $\wedge$ H(H(a).b).c = 1}.

\infrule[I-ValueField]{
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\{l$_1$:v$_1$,$\ldots$,l$_n$:v$_n$\}}}
}{\mbox{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$.l}
}}

\inferax{I-Ref}{\inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{r}{\Env}{true}{r}}

\inferrule{I-Op}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_a}$}{e$_a$} \andalso
  \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_b}$}{e$_b$}
}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\oplus$ e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_a}$$\wedge$e$_{C_b}$}{e$_a$ $\oplus$ e$_b$}
}

% \inferrule{I-Compare}{
%   \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_a}$}{e$_a$} \andalso
%   \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_b}$}{e$_b$}
% }{
%   \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\olessthan$ e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_a}$$\wedge$e$_{C_b}$}{e$_a$ $\olessthan$ e$_b$}
% }

% \inferrule{I-Combine}{
%   \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_a}$}{e$_a$} \andalso
%   \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_b}$}{e$_b$}
% }{
%   \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ $\ovee$ e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_a}$$\wedge$e$_{C_b}$}{e$_a$ $\ovee$ e$_b$}
% }

\inferrule{I-Identity}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_a}$}{e$_a$} \andalso
  \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_b}$}{e$_b$}
}{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$ == e$_2$}{\Env$''$}{e$_{C_a}$$\wedge$e$_{C_b}$}{e$_a$ == e$_b$}
}

The \textsc{I-Identity} rule ensures that identities cannot change in
value constraint expressions. Because we inline the expressions on
either side, these, through \textsc{I-Field} and
\textsc{I-ValueField}, are forced to stay as they are up to the last
part. Note that that inlined identity constraints do not type (in
\textsc{S-Once}), even if both operands have the same type --- this
prevents adding identity constraints mixed with value constraints.

\infrule[I-Call]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{$\cdots$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{e$_C$} = (\texttt{e=v $\wedge$ e$_1$=v$_1$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ e$_n$=v$_n$})}\\
  \mbox{
    \lookup{v}{l}{s; return e}}\\
  \mbox{
    \enter{\Env$_n$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$'$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v$_r$}}\\
}{\mbox{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$'''$}{e$_C$}{v$_r$}
}}

Methods that have any statements at all can only be used in a one-way
manner. This is ensured by evaluating the return expression and using
only the value in the constraint. Because we are retranslating all
constraints on each semantic step, this return value will get updated
when its dependencies change, it just won't work in the other
direction.

When inlining a method with more than one statement, the statements
are simply executed.  In particular, this means that we eagerly choose
which branch of if-statements to inline and eagerly unroll loops.
Further, the \textsc{I-Call} rule above and the \textsc{I-MultiWayCall} rule
below ensure that methods being used in constraints have no side
effects.  This is accomplished by requiring the initial heap to remain
unchanged.

Similarly, methods used in constraints cannot declare nested
constraints; this is accomplished by requiring the initial sets of
ordinary and identity constraints to remain unchanged.

\infrule[I-MultiWayCall]{
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_0}$}{e$_0'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$}{\Env$''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \lookup{v}{l}{return e}}\\
  \mbox{
    \enter{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$'''$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{e$_{C_1}$}{e$_1'$}
    $\cdots$
    \inline{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{e$_{C_n}$}{e$_n'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{\Scope$_m$}(\texttt{self}) = \texttt{x$_{g_\texttt{self}}$} \andalso
    \texttt{\Scope$_m$}(\texttt{x$_1$}) = \texttt{x$_{g_1}$} $\cdots$
    \texttt{\Scope$_m$}(\texttt{x$_n$}) = \texttt{x$_{g_n}$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{e$_C$} = (\texttt{x$_{g_\texttt{self}}$=e$_0'$ $\wedge$ x$_{g_1}$=e$_1'$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ x$_{g_n}$=e$_n'$})}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$_n$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$_n'$}{e$_{C_m}$}{e$'$}}
}{\mbox{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$_n'$}{e$_C$$\wedge$e$_{C_m}$$\wedge$e$_{C_0}$$\wedge$e$_{C_1}$$\wedge\cdots\wedge$e$_{C_n}$}{e$'$}
}}

For methods that only return an expression, we inline the expression
and pass it to the solver. Note that we execute the argument
expressions and receiver for their value (potentially executing
through other methods), and also inline them, potentially executing
the same methods twice (once through \textsc{I-Call} and once through
\textsc{E-Call}).  Although not ideal in terms of providing the
cleanest possible semantics, in practical terms this should 
not be a problem, because we prohibit
side-effects in these methods.


% \judgment{\reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{\Env$'$}{C}}
% \judgment{\reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\Env$'$}{C}}

% ``Re-inlining the constraint store \Cstore\ returns a
% constraint \texttt{C}''

% ``Re-inlining the constraint store \Istore\ returns a
% constraint \texttt{C}''

We use the below rules to re-translate all constraints in the store using our
inlining rules before solving.

\inferax{I-ReinlineEmptyC}{\reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{$\emptyset$}{\Env}{true}}

\inferax{I-ReinlineEmptyI}{\reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{$\emptyset$}{\Env}{true}}

\infrule[I-ReinlineC]{
  \mbox{
    \Cstore$_0$ = \Cstore $\;\setminus$ $\{(\texttt{\Scope}, \rho\, \texttt{e})\}$ \andalso
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Env$_0$}{C$_0$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore$_0$}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_e}$}{e$'$}}
}{\mbox{
  \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{\Env$'$}{C$_0$ $\wedge$ $\rho$ (e$'$ $\wedge$ e$_{C_e}$)}
}}

\infrule[I-ReinlineI]{
  \mbox{
    \Istore$_0$ = \Istore $\;\setminus$ $\{(\texttt{\Scope}, \texttt{required\, e})\}$ \andalso
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore$_0$}{\Env$_0$}{C$_0$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore$_0$}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_e}$}{e$'$}}
}{\mbox{
  \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\Env$'$}{C$_0$ $\wedge$ required (e$'$ $\wedge$ e$_{C_e}$)}
}}


% \judgment{\solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}}
% \judgment{\solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}}

The rules below are helpers for use in solving constraints. They generate the
inlined constraint expression from \texttt{e}, re-inline the constraints from
the identity respectively constraint stores, and to generate the stay
constraints and solve for these. In the first phase, we use the first judgment
above to inline the identity constraint store and generate the stays on the heap
and environment such that the solve can propagate the new equality constraint
through all existing identity constraints in order to update other variables and
fields as needed.  In the second phase we use the second judgment above generate
constraints for the non-identity constraint store, and we typecheck under the
new environment before solving.

\infrule[SolveIdentityConstraints]{
  \mbox{
    \gstay{\Env}{weak}{C$_{E_s}$} \andalso
    \gstay{\Heap}{weak}{C$_{{H}_s}$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{\Env$_i$}{C$_i$} \andalso
  }\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_{e}$}{e$_c$}{e$'$} \andalso
    \solveH{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{C$_i$ $\wedge$ $\rho$ e$_C$}
  }
}{\mbox{
  \solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}
}}

\infrule[SolveValueConstraints]{
  \mbox{
    \gstay{\Env}{required}{C$_{E_s}$} \andalso
    \gstay{\Heap}{required}{C$_{{H}_s}$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \reinline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{\Env$_c$}{C$_c$} \andalso
  }\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_{e}$}{e$_c$}{e$'$} \andalso
    \tcH{\Env$_{e}$}{\Heap}{e$'$}{T} \andalso
    \solveH{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{C$_c$ $\wedge$ $\rho$ e$_C$}
  }
}{\mbox{
  \solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}
}}

% \judgment{\stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}}

The statement rules are refactored to work with the local environments
and the new constraint and identity-constraint stores.

\infrule[S-AsgnNewLocal]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x})$\uparrow$ \andalso
    \texttt{\Env}(\texttt{x$_g$})$\uparrow$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{\Scope}' = \texttt{\Scope} \bigcup \{(\texttt{x},\texttt{x}_g)\}$ \andalso
    $\texttt{\Env}'' = \texttt{\Env}' \bigcup \{(\texttt{x}_g,\texttt{v})\}$}\\
}{\mbox{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x := e}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}
}}

\infrule[S-AsgnLocal]{
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{x}_g$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \solveConstraints{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{\texttt{required}}{x$_g$=v}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$'''$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \solveConstraints{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{\Heap$''$}{\Istore$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\texttt{required}}{x$_g$=v}{\Env$'''$}{\Heap$'''$}}
}{\mbox{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x := e}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'''$}{\Cstore}{\Istore}
}}

\infrule[S-AsgnLValue]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \solveConstraints{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{\texttt{required}}{e$_l$.l=v}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$''$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \solveConstraints{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{\Heap$''$}{\Istore$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\texttt{required}}{e$_l$.l=v}{\Env$'''$}{\Heap$'''$}}
}{\mbox{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_l$.l := e}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'''$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}
}}

Note that the above rule can only be used in constraint-construction
mode if \Heap$ = $\Heap$'$\@. % This effectively restricts assignments
% in constraints to work on values. Note also, that assignments in
% constraints are just executed, but do not set up a required equality
% constraint between the LHS and RHS\@.
% (The current Babelsberg/Ruby
% includes a ``backwards compatibility mode'' which sets up required
% equality constraints when assignment occurs, but it's not clear
% whether we should retain support for that.  See
% \autoref{sec:constraint-construction-impl}.)
We also do not allow the use of the following rules for \texttt{once} and
\texttt{always} in constraint-construction mode, because the inlining
rules disallow updating the constraint store and heap.  As discussed
previously (Section~\ref{sec:additional-constraint-restrictions}),
a practical implementation we might want to support benign side
effects in methods that are invoked by constraint expressions,
including methods that themselves create new constraints; but this is
not modeled by this semantics.


\infrule[S-OnceIdentity]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$}{\Env$_0$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v} \andalso
    \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$_1$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$}{\Env$_2$}{e$_{C_0}$}{e$_0'$} \andalso
    \inline{\Env$_2$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_3$}{e$_{C_1}$}{e$_1'$}}\\
}{\mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{once e$_0$ == e$_1$}{\Env$_3$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}
}}

Note that the above rule uses the inlining judgment to ensure that the
expressions adhere to our restrictions on what kind of expressions can
appear in constraints. Expressions that update the heap, for example,
will be rejected in the inlining rules.

\infrule[S-AlwaysIdentity]{
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{once e$_0$ == e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
  \mbox{
    \Istore$'$ = \Istore $\;\bigcup$ $\{($\texttt{\Scope}$,\,$\texttt{e}$_0$ \texttt{==} \texttt{e}$_1)\}$}
}{\mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{always e$_0$ == e$_1$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore}{\Istore$'$}
}}

\infrule[S-Once]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{C$_0$} = $\rho$ \texttt{e} \andalso
    \solveConstraints{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Istore}{\Cstore}{$\rho$}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}
  }
}{\mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{once C$_0$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}
}

\infrule[S-Always]{
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{once C$_0$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
  \mbox{
    \Cstore$'$ = \Cstore $\;\bigcup$ $\{(\texttt{\Scope}, \texttt{C}_0)\}$}
}
{\mbox{\stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{always C$_0$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore}}}

\inferax{S-Skip}{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{skip}{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}
}

\infrule[S-Seq]{
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s$_1$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}}
}{\mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s$_1$;s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}
}}

\infrule[S-IfThen]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{true}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{s$_1$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}}
}{\mbox{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{if e then s$_1$ else s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}%
}}

\infrule[S-IfElse]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{false}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}}
}{\mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{if e then s$_1$ else s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}%
}}

\infrule[S-WhileDo]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{true}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{s}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \stepHO{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}{while e do s}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$''$}{\Heap$'''$}{\Cstore$'''$}{\Istore$'''$}}
}{\mbox{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{while e do s}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$''$}{\Heap$'''$}{\Cstore$'''$}{\Istore$'''$}
}}

\inferrule{S-WhileSkip}{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{false}
}{
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{while e do s}{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}
}

\subsection{Key Properties}
\label{sec:proofs}

In prior publications, we have presented and proven two key properties of our
formalism for a simpler language, called Babelsberg/UID~\cite{FelgentreffOOPSLA2015,felgentreff:2015:oopsla-companion}. Here we state
the same two key theorems about our formalism, updated for the
Babelsberg/Objects language presented here. The first theorem formalizes the
idea that any solution to a value constraint preserves the structures of the
objects on the environment and heap:

\begin{sloppypar}
\begin{theorem} (Structure Preservation)
 If \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{(once|always)
   C$_0$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$} and  \tcHC{\Env}{\Heap}{C$_0$} and
\tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{x}{T}, then
  \tcH{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{x}{T}. 
\end{theorem}

The second theorem formalizes the idea that all solutions to an assignment will
produce structurally equivalent environments and heaps (provided we start in a
well-formed configuration where all identity constraints are satisfied):

\begin{theorem} (Structural Determinism)
  If
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{L := e}{\Env$_1$}{\Scope$_2$}{\Heap$_1$}{\Cstore$_1$}{\Istore$_1$} and
  \stepHO{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{L := e}{\Env$_2$}{\Scope$_2$}{\Heap$_2$}{\Cstore$_2$}{\Istore$_2$} and
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{I}{true} and 
  \texttt{x} in dom(\Env),
  then
  \tcH{\Env$_1$}{\Heap$_1$}{x}{T} and
  \tcH{\Env$_2$}{\Heap$_2$}{x}{T}.
  % $\forall \texttt{x}\in dom(\Env_1):$ \tcH{\Env$_1$}{\Heap$_1$}{x}{T} $\Rightarrow$
  % \tcH{\Env$_2$}{\Heap$_2$}{x}{T}.
\end{theorem}
\end{sloppypar}

\section{Collections of Objects}
\label{sec:records-with-ident}

In this final extension to Babelsberg/Objects, we support a limited number of
predicates on collections. These additional rules illustrate the facilities to
support higher-order functions in this design. The semantics are a straight
extension from the full object-oriented language, and we present only the
additional rules and syntax.

\subsection{Syntax}

The syntax is augmented to include an element \ocollpred, which ranges
over the core predicates on collections such as \verb!every!,
\verb!some!, \verb!member!. For languages which support re-definition
of the methods that come with the language, we assume that the element
matches only the original implementations, not user-defined
re-definitions. Furthermore, we augment the syntax to also support
accessing records using expressions.

{\texttt{
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\textrm{L-Value} & L & ::= & x $\mid$ e.l $\mid$ e[e] \\
\textrm{Label} & l & ::= & \textrm{record label names} $\mid$ \ocollpred
\end{tabular}
}}\\[\baselineskip]

\subsection{Semantics}

\begin{table*}
  \caption{Judgments and Intuitions of Additional and Changed Semantic Rules}
  \label{tab:collection-judgments}
  \centering
  \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{r X}
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Opaque Judgments}}}\\
    $asLabel(\texttt{c}) = \texttt{l}$ & Constant \texttt{c} converted into a label yields \texttt{l}\\
    \isArray{T}{c} & When type \texttt{T} corresponds to a known basic collection type
                     that is supported in constraints with predicates, \texttt{c} is
                     \texttt{true}.\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Constraint Solving}}}\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_0$}{e$'$}}\\
                                       & Inlining expression \texttt{e} in \texttt{\Scope} is equivalent to
                                         \texttt{e$'$} in \Env if \texttt{e$_C$} evaluates to true.\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_0$}{e$_c$}{e$_d$}}\\
                                       & Inlining statement \texttt{s} is equivalent to solving 
                                         conjunction of constraint expressions \texttt{e$_C$} and the
                                         disjunction of constraint expressions \texttt{e$_D$} if \texttt{e$_0$} evaluates
                                         to true. This inlining step returns an updated environment
                                         \texttt{\Env$'$} and scope \texttt{\Scope$'$}.\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{\textit{Helper Rule}}}\\
    \multicolumn{2}{l}{$preparePredicate(\Env,\Scope,\Heap,\Cstore,\Istore,$\texttt{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}$) = (\Env',\Scope',\texttt{s; return c},\texttt{e}_C)$}\\
                                       & Preparing the method call \texttt{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)} for inlining
                                         returns and updated environment \Env$'$, the fresh method scope \Scope$'$,
                                         the method body \texttt{s; return c}, and is valid if \texttt{e$_C$} evaluates
                                         to true.
  \end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

We add two opaque helper judgments. The first converts constants to label names,
and the second checks if a constant value refers to an array class type. Both
are defined in terms of the host language \ac{api}. Note that for languages the
support re-definition of core classes, the second judgment will return
\verb!false! if such re-definition has taken place.

Besides those additions, we only add the extended evaluation rules for dynamic
field access and the special \gls{ccm} for collection \acp{api}.  Note that we
do not add a typing rule for dynamic field access --- during inlining, such
access are turned into ordinary field accesses, and their expressions are
required to stay equal to the current value.

\infrule[E-ExpField]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_l$}{\Env$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{c} \andalso
    $asLabel(\texttt{c}) = \texttt{l}$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap$'$}{\Cstore$'$}{\Istore$'$}{e.l}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}{v}}\\
}{\mbox{
  \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e[e$_l$]}{\Env$''$}{\Heap$''$}{\Cstore$''$}{\Istore$''$}{v}
}}

\infrule[I-ExpField]{
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_l$}{\Env$'$}{e$_{C_l}$}{e$_l'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_l$}{\Env$''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{c} \andalso
    $asLabel(\texttt{c}) = \texttt{l}$}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.l}{\Env$'''$}{e$_C$}{e$'$}}
}{\mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e[e$_l$]}{\Env$'''$}{e$_{C_l}$$\wedge$e$_C$$\wedge$e$_l'$=c}{e$'$}
}}

We extend the inlining judgment to also work for statements. This is
used in the inlining judgment to translate calls to the well-known
collection predicates. These predicates will not match the previous
\textsc{I-MultiWayCall} rule, because their implementations have more
than a single return expression, so that rule is unchanged. Because we
allow a limited subset of statements, including assignment to locals
in inlining collection predicates, the inlining rule including
statements also returns an updated scope. In addition, the constraint
expressions that are returned by the inlining rule for statements are
split into groups for conjunctions and disjunctions --- this is
required to track, based on the early returns that are encountered,
whether a set of inlined expressions all need to be satisfied or if
just one needs to be satisfied.

We define a helper judgments to inline collection predicates:
\infrule[PreparePredicate]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_0$}{\Env$''$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}}\\
  \mbox{
    \tcH{\Env}{\Heap}{v}{T} \andalso \isArray{T}{true}}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_0$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_1$}{\Env$_1$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v$_1$}}\\
  \mbox{$\cdots$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env$_{n-1}$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e$_n$}{\Env$_n$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v$_n$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \texttt{e$_C$} = (\texttt{e=v $\wedge$ e$_1$=v$_1$ $\wedge$ $\cdots$ $\wedge$ e$_n$=v$_n$})}\\
  \mbox{
    \lookup{v}{l}{s; return c}}\\
  \mbox{
    \enter{\Env$_n$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$_m$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}}\\
}{\mbox{
    $preparePredicate(\Env,\Scope,\Heap,\Cstore,\Istore,$\texttt{e.l(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}$) = (\Env',\Scope_m,\texttt{s; return c},\texttt{e}_C)$
}}

This helper rule sets up the required equalities for all the arguments
and the receiver, and is essentially the same as \textsc{I-Call}. As
an addition, it limits any inlining to collection predicates that
return a constant as a final statement. In the two rules that follow,
this constant is further limited to be either \verb!true! or
\verb!false!.

\infrule[I-PositivePredicate]{
  \mbox{
    $preparePredicate(\Env,\Scope,\Heap,\Cstore,\Istore,$\texttt{e.\ocollpred(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}$) = (\Env',\Scope',\texttt{s; return c},\texttt{e}_0)$}\\
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{c} = \texttt{true}$ \andalso
    \inlineC{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_1$}{e$_C$}{e$_D$}
  }\\
}{\mbox{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.\ocollpred(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$$\wedge$e$_1$}{$\texttt{e}_C$}
}}

\infrule[I-NegativePredicate]{
  \mbox{
    $preparePredicate(\Env,\Scope,\Heap,\Cstore,\Istore,$\texttt{e.\ocollpred(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}$) = (\Env',\Scope',\texttt{s; return c},\texttt{e}_0)$}\\
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{c} = \texttt{false}$ \andalso
    \inlineC{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_1$}{e$_C$}{e$_D$}
  }\\
}{\mbox{
  \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e.\ocollpred(e$_1$,$\ldots$,e$_n$)}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$$\wedge$e$_1$}{$\texttt{e}_C \wedge \texttt{e}_D$}
}}

We use two separate rules for inlining through collection predicates
that return \verb!true! or \verb!false! as their final statement. For
methods that return \verb!true! any disjunction, which would be
created by an early return true, does not have to be fulfilled, as
even without the early return the method would return
true. Conversely, when the method returns \verb!false!, fulfilling any
conjunction will not suffice, because that would simply prevent an
early return \verb!false!, but not the final return statement.

Since we now allow inlining through a limited subset of statements, we
add inlining rules for those. Note that these rules can only come into
play through an \textsc{I-*Predicate}. Furthermore, all rules not
supplied here still lead to a failure to evaluate an
\textsc{I-*Predicate} rule, and fall back to the previous
\textsc{I-Call} rule to set up a one-way constraint on the result of
the call.

\infrule[I-AsgnNewLocal]{
  \mbox{
    \texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x})$\uparrow$ \andalso
    \texttt{\Env}(\texttt{x$_g$})$\uparrow$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v} \andalso
    \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$}{e$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{\Scope}' = \texttt{\Scope} \bigcup \{(\texttt{x},\texttt{x}_g)\}$ \andalso
    $\texttt{\Env}''' = \texttt{\Env}'' \bigcup \{(\texttt{x}_g,\texttt{v})\}$}\\
}{\mbox{
  \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x := e}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_0$$\wedge$e$'$=v$\wedge$x$_g$=v}{true}{false}
}}

Assignments are only permitted to local variables. Since we can only
start the statement inlining rules from a collection predicate
\ocollpred, we start with a fresh scope and any local variable must be
newly created first. In this case, assignment is turned into a
required equality between the fresh variable name and the initial
value. Note that we are using the expression judgment to evaluate the
right-hand side, but we disallow any changes to the heap or the
constraint stores.

\infrule[I-AsgnLocal]{
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{\Scope}(\texttt{x}) = \texttt{x}_g$ \andalso
    \texttt{\Env}(\texttt{x$_g'$})$\uparrow$}\\
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{v} \andalso
    \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$}{e$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    $\texttt{\Scope}' = \texttt{\Scope} \setminus \{\texttt{x}, \texttt{x}_g\}$ \andalso
    $\texttt{\Scope}'' = \texttt{\Scope}' \bigcup \{(\texttt{x},\texttt{x}_g')\}$ \andalso
    $\texttt{\Env}''' = \texttt{\Env}'' \bigcup \{(\texttt{x}_g',\texttt{v})\}$}\\
}{\mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{x := e}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$''$}{e$_0$$\wedge$e$'$=v$\wedge$x$_g'$=v}{true}{false}
}}

Since we do not allow creating additional constraints even in this
extended inlining mode, there is no need to solve constraints when we
re-assign to a local variable. Furthermore, since re-assignments are
needed for looping over collection indices, and these indices are also
used to then access the collection, we create a fresh global name for
every re-assigned variable. This way, every re-assignment turns into a
new variable for the solver.

\inferax{I-Skip}{\inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{skip}{\Env}{\Scope}{true}{true}{true}}

\infrule[I-Seq]{
  \mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s$_1$}{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_1$}{e$_{C1}$}{e$_{D1}$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env$'$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$''$}{e$_2$}{e$_{C2}$}{e$_{D2}$}}\\
}{\mbox{
  \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s$_1$;s$_2$}{\Env$''$}{\Scope$''$}{$\texttt{e}_{1}\wedge\texttt{e}_{2}$}{$\texttt{e}_{C1}\wedge\texttt{e}_{C2}$}{$\texttt{e}_{D1}\vee\texttt{e}_{D2}$}
}}

The skip and sequence rules are straightforward. The conjunction and
disjunction expressions from the sequences are connected
appropriately.

\infrule[I-IfThenReturnTrue]{\mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$}
}}{\mbox{
  \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{if e then return true else s}{\Env}{\Scope}{e$_C$}{true}{e$'$}%
}}

\infrule[I-IfThenReturnFalse]{\mbox{
    \inline{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{e$_C$}{e$'$}
}}{\mbox{
  \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{if e then return false else s}{\Env}{\Scope}{e$_C$}{e$'$=false}{false}%
}}

We only support if-clauses used as early returns in this extended
inlining mode. As described in \autoref{sec:constr-coll-pred}, if the
early return would return \verb!true!, the inlined conditional is used
in a disjunction, otherwise it is used in a conjunction.

\infrule[I-WhileDo]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{true}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$}{e$'$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{s}{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$'$}{e$_1$}{e$_{C_1}$}{e$_{D_1}$}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env$'''$}{\Scope$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{while e do s}{\Env$''''$}{\Scope$''$}{e$_r$}{e$_{C_r}$}{e$_{D_r}$}}
}{\mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{while e do s}{\Env$''''$}{\Scope$''$}{%
      e$_0$$\wedge$e$'$$\wedge$e$_1$$\wedge$e$_r$}{%
      e$_{C_0}$$\wedge$e$_{C_r}$}{%
      e$_{D_0}$$\vee$e$_{D_r}$}
}}

\infrule[I-WhileSkip]{
  \mbox{
    \evalBig{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$'$}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{false}}\\
  \mbox{
    \inline{\Env$'$}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{e}{\Env$''$}{e$_0$}{e$'$}}\\
}{\mbox{
    \inlineC{\Env}{\Scope}{\Heap}{\Cstore}{\Istore}{while e do s}{\Env$''$}{\Scope}{e$_0$$\wedge$e$'$=false}{true}{false}
}}

Finally, the while construct is now supported during inlining. Note
that the loop condition is inlined and required to stay at its
value. This prevents the solver from being able to change the loop
condition to, for example, satisfy the collection predicate only on a
subset of the collection.

\ParSep

There is a noteworthy problem with these rules: they may generate
constraints that are too strong. Consider the following method, which
tests if either at least one element in the array is larger than ten,
or else all elements are negative:
\begin{lstlisting}
def some_or_none()
  i := 0;
  while i < self.length do (
    if self[i] > 10 then return true;
    if self[i] < 0 then return false;
    i := i + 1
  );
  return true
end

always array.some_or_none()
\end{lstlisting}
Here, the constraint would be satisfied if:
\[
  \exists x\in \texttt{array}. x > 10 \vee \forall x\in \texttt{array}. x < 0
\]
